\noindent This document contains all 70 \emph{Curve Sketching} questions, each with a fully worked
solution and a TikZ sketch of the curve, plotted from its equation. Roots, intercepts, turning points and
points of inflection are marked, and asymptotes are drawn as dashed grey lines. (The few questions that
only ask you to \emph{describe} or \emph{match} shapes, with several functions at once, have no single plot.)

\section*{Curve Sketching 01\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-001)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 01 (cg5-001)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{State the general shape of the graph of each of the following functions, giving a reason in each case. \\[3pt] a) \( y = 4x^5 \) \\[3pt] b) \( y = -2x^4 \) \\[3pt] c) \( y = -7x^3 \) \\[3pt] d) \( y = 3x^6 \)}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: For \( y = 4x^5 \): odd power, positive coefficient.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Power } 5 \text{ is odd, } k = 4 > 0
\end{gathered}\]
Odd power with positive \( k \) gives a bottom-left to top-right shape.

\textit{Step 2: For \( y = -2x^4 \): even power, negative coefficient.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Power } 4 \text{ is even, } k = -2 < 0
\end{gathered}\]
Even power with negative \( k \) gives an n-shape (opening downward).

\textit{Step 3: For \( y = -7x^3 \): odd power, negative coefficient.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Power } 3 \text{ is odd, } k = -7 < 0
\end{gathered}\]
Odd power with negative \( k \) gives a top-left to bottom-right shape.

\textit{Step 4: For \( y = 3x^6 \): even power, positive coefficient.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Power } 6 \text{ is even, } k = 3 > 0
\end{gathered}\]
Even power with positive \( k \) gives a u-shape (opening upward).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad a) Bottom-left to top-right; b) n-shape; c) Top-left to bottom-right; d) u-shape
\end{answerbox}
\medskip\hrule

\section*{Curve Sketching 02\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-002)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 02 (cg5-002)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Four graphs are shown below, labelled A, B, C, and D. Match each graph to the correct function. \\[3pt] - Graph A: u-shape entirely above the \(x\)-axis, passing through the origin. - Graph B: top-left to bottom-right, passing through the origin. - Graph C: n-shape entirely below the \(x\)-axis, passing through the origin. - Graph D: bottom-left to top-right, passing through the origin. \\[3pt] Functions: \( y = 0.5x^3 \), \( y = -4x^2 \), \( y = 2x^4 \), \( y = -3x^5 \)}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Graph A is a u-shape: even power, positive coefficient.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 2x^4 \quad (\text{even, positive})
\end{gathered}\]
\( x^4 \) is even so gives a u/n-shape; positive coefficient means u-shape.

\textit{Step 2: Graph B is top-left to bottom-right: odd power, negative coefficient.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = -3x^5 \quad (\text{odd, negative})
\end{gathered}\]
Odd power with negative \( k \) gives top-left to bottom-right.

\textit{Step 3: Graph C is n-shape: even power, negative coefficient.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = -4x^2 \quad (\text{even, negative})
\end{gathered}\]
Even power with negative \( k \) gives an n-shape below the axis.

\textit{Step 4: Graph D is bottom-left to top-right: odd power, positive coefficient.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 0.5x^3 \quad (\text{odd, positive})
\end{gathered}\]
Odd power with positive \( k \) gives bottom-left to top-right.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle A \leftrightarrow y = 2x^4\); \(\displaystyle B \leftrightarrow y = -3x^5\); \(\displaystyle C \leftrightarrow y = -4x^2\); \(\displaystyle D \leftrightarrow y = 0.5x^3\)
\end{answerbox}
\medskip\hrule

\section*{Curve Sketching 03\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-003)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 03 (cg5-003)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Sketch the graph of \( y = x(x - 2)(x + 3) \), clearly showing where the curve meets the coordinate axes.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Identify the leading term by expanding partially.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = x \cdot x \cdot x + \cdots = x^3 + \cdots
\end{gathered}\]
The highest power is \( x^3 \) with a positive coefficient, so the shape is bottom-left to top-right.

\textit{Step 2: Find the \( y \)-intercept by substituting \( x = 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 0(0-2)(0+3) = 0
\end{gathered}\]
The curve passes through the origin.

\textit{Step 3: Find the \( x \)-intercepts by setting each factor to zero.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 0, \quad x - 2 = 0 \Rightarrow x = 2, \quad x + 3 = 0 \Rightarrow x = -3
\end{gathered}\]
Three distinct roots at \( x = -3, 0, 2 \).

\textit{Step 4: Describe the sketch.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Crosses } x\text{-axis at } {-3,\ 0,\ 2};\quad y\text{-intercept at } 0
\end{gathered}\]
The curve crosses all three roots (no repeated factors), rising from bottom-left to top-right overall.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Cubic crossing \(\displaystyle x\)-axis at \(\displaystyle x = -3, 0, 2\); passes through origin; positive leading coefficient.
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}

\section*{Curve Sketching 04\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-004)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 04 (cg5-004)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Sketch the graph of \( y = (x + 1)(x - 3)(x - 5) \), showing clearly where the curve meets the axes.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Determine the overall shape.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = x^3 + \cdots \quad (\text{positive leading coefficient})
\end{gathered}\]
Bottom-left to top-right shape.

\textit{Step 2: Find the \( y \)-intercept.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = (1)(-3)(-5) = 15
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( x = 0 \): the three factors give \( 1 \times (-3) \times (-5) = 15 \).

\textit{Step 3: Find the \( x \)-intercepts.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x + 1 = 0 \Rightarrow x = -1; \quad x - 3 = 0 \Rightarrow x = 3; \quad x - 5 = 0 \Rightarrow x = 5
\end{gathered}\]
Three distinct roots at \( x = -1, 3, 5 \).

\textit{Step 4: Describe the sketch.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Crosses at } x = -1,\ 3,\ 5; \quad y\text{-intercept at } (0, 15)
\end{gathered}\]
The curve crosses each root cleanly, with \( y \)-intercept at 15.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Cubic crossing \(\displaystyle x\)-axis at \(\displaystyle -1, 3, 5\); \(\displaystyle y\)-intercept at \(\displaystyle (0, 15)\).
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}

\section*{Curve Sketching 05\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-005)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 05 (cg5-005)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Sketch the graph of \( y = -x(x + 2)(x - 4) \), showing where the curve meets the axes.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Identify the leading term.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = -x \cdot x \cdot x + \cdots = -x^3 + \cdots
\end{gathered}\]
Negative coefficient of \( x^3 \) means top-left to bottom-right shape.

\textit{Step 2: Find the \( y \)-intercept.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = -(0)(2)(-4) = 0
\end{gathered}\]
The curve passes through the origin.

\textit{Step 3: Find the \( x \)-intercepts.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 0, \quad x = -2, \quad x = 4
\end{gathered}\]
Each factor set to zero gives a distinct root.

\textit{Step 4: Describe the sketch.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Crosses at } x = -2,\ 0,\ 4; \quad \text{top-left to bottom-right overall}
\end{gathered}\]
Negative leading coefficient flips the orientation compared to a standard cubic.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Cubic crossing \(\displaystyle x\)-axis at \(\displaystyle x = -2, 0, 4\); passes through origin; top-left to bottom-right shape.
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}

\section*{Curve Sketching 06\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-006)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 06 (cg5-006)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{a) Factorise completely \( x^3 + x^2 - 6x \). \\[3pt] b) Hence sketch the graph of \( y = x^3 + x^2 - 6x \), showing where it meets the coordinate axes.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Part a: take out the common factor \( x \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^3 + x^2 - 6x = x(x^2 + x - 6)
\end{gathered}\]
Every term has a factor of \( x \).

\textit{Step 2: Factorise the quadratic \( x^2 + x - 6 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 + x - 6 = (x + 3)(x - 2)
\end{gathered}\]
We need two numbers that multiply to \(-6\) and add to \(1\): these are \(3\) and \(-2\).

\textit{Step 3: Write the full factorisation.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x(x + 3)(x - 2)
\end{gathered}\]
Three linear factors, so three roots.

\textit{Step 4: Part b: find \( x \)-intercepts and \( y \)-intercept.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 0,\ -3,\ 2; \quad y(0) = 0
\end{gathered}\]
All three roots are distinct; the \( y \)-intercept is at the origin.

\textit{Step 5: Describe the sketch.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Positive }x^3\text{ coefficient: bottom-left to top-right}
\end{gathered}\]
The curve crosses all three roots and rises to the right.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad a) \(\displaystyle x(x+3)(x-2)\); b) Cubic crossing at \(\displaystyle x = -3, 0, 2\); positive shape.
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}

\section*{Curve Sketching 07\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-007)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 07 (cg5-007)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Sketch the graph of \( y = (2x + 1)(x - 2)(x - 4) \), showing where the curve meets the axes.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Find the leading coefficient.}
\[\begin{gathered}
2x \cdot x \cdot x = 2x^3 \quad (\text{positive})
\end{gathered}\]
Positive leading coefficient: bottom-left to top-right shape.

\textit{Step 2: Find the \( y \)-intercept.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = (1)(-2)(-4) = 8
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( x = 0 \) into each factor.

\textit{Step 3: Find the \( x \)-intercepts.}
\[\begin{gathered}
2x + 1 = 0 \Rightarrow x = -\tfrac{1}{2}; \quad x = 2; \quad x = 4
\end{gathered}\]
Three distinct roots including a fractional one.

\textit{Step 4: Describe the sketch.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Crosses at } x = -\tfrac{1}{2},\ 2,\ 4; \quad y\text{-intercept at } (0, 8)
\end{gathered}\]
The curve crosses all three roots cleanly.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Cubic crossing \(\displaystyle x\)-axis at \(\displaystyle x = -\tfrac{1}{2}, 2, 4\); \(\displaystyle y\)-intercept at \(\displaystyle (0, 8)\).
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}

\section*{Curve Sketching 08\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-008)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 08 (cg5-008)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{a) Factorise completely \( y = 2x^3 - 8x \). \\[3pt] b) Hence sketch the graph, showing where it meets the coordinate axes.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Part a: take out the common factor.}
\[\begin{gathered}
2x^3 - 8x = 2x(x^2 - 4)
\end{gathered}\]
Factor out \( 2x \).

\textit{Step 2: Factorise \( x^2 - 4 \) as a difference of two squares.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 - 4 = (x-2)(x+2)
\end{gathered}\]
\( a^2 - b^2 = (a-b)(a+b) \) with \( a = x, b = 2 \).

\textit{Step 3: Write the full factorisation.}
\[\begin{gathered}
2x(x - 2)(x + 2)
\end{gathered}\]
Three distinct linear factors.

\textit{Step 4: Part b: roots and \( y \)-intercept.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 0,\ 2,\ -2; \quad y(0) = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Symmetrically placed roots at \( \pm 2 \) and at the origin.

\textit{Step 5: Describe the sketch.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Positive leading coefficient: bottom-left to top-right}
\end{gathered}\]
The graph is symmetric about the origin (odd function) and crosses at \( -2, 0, 2 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad a) \(\displaystyle 2x(x-2)(x+2)\); b) Cubic crossing at \(\displaystyle x = -2, 0, 2\); passes through origin.
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}

\section*{Curve Sketching 09\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-009)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 09 (cg5-009)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Sketch the graph of \( y = x^2(x - 3) \), showing clearly where the curve meets the axes and describing the behaviour at each \( x \)-intercept.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Identify the shape from the leading term.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = x^3 - 3x^2 \implies \text{positive }x^3
\end{gathered}\]
Bottom-left to top-right overall shape.

\textit{Step 2: Find the \( y \)-intercept.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 0^2(0-3) = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Passes through the origin.

\textit{Step 3: Find the \( x \)-intercepts and their nature.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 = 0 \Rightarrow x = 0 \;(\text{double root}); \quad x - 3 = 0 \Rightarrow x = 3 \;(\text{single root})
\end{gathered}\]
A double root means the curve touches the \( x \)-axis at \( x = 0 \) without crossing it.

\textit{Step 4: Describe the behaviour at each root.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 0: \text{ touches; } \quad x = 3: \text{ crosses}
\end{gathered}\]
The curve bounces off the axis at \( x = 0 \) and cuts through at \( x = 3 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Cubic touching \(\displaystyle x\)-axis at \(\displaystyle x = 0\) (double root) and crossing at \(\displaystyle x = 3\); \(\displaystyle y\)-intercept at origin.
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}

\section*{Curve Sketching 10\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-010)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 10 (cg5-010)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Sketch the graph of \( y = (x + 2)^2(x - 1) \), showing where the curve meets the axes.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Identify the leading term.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 \cdot x = x^3 \quad (\text{positive coefficient})
\end{gathered}\]
Bottom-left to top-right shape.

\textit{Step 2: Find the \( y \)-intercept.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = (0+2)^2(0-1) = 4 \times (-1) = -4
\end{gathered}\]
The curve crosses the \( y \)-axis at \( -4 \).

\textit{Step 3: Find the \( x \)-intercepts.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x+2)^2 = 0 \Rightarrow x = -2 \;(\text{double}); \quad x - 1 = 0 \Rightarrow x = 1 \;(\text{single})
\end{gathered}\]
The curve touches at \( x = -2 \) and crosses at \( x = 1 \).

\textit{Step 4: Describe the sketch.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Touches at } x = -2, \text{ crosses at } x = 1, \; y\text{-intercept at } (0,-4)
\end{gathered}\]
The curve dips down to touch the axis at \( -2 \) and rises to cross at \( x = 1 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Cubic touching \(\displaystyle x\)-axis at \(\displaystyle x = -2\), crossing at \(\displaystyle x = 1\); \(\displaystyle y\)-intercept at \(\displaystyle (0, -4)\).
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}

\section*{Curve Sketching 11\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-011)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 11 (cg5-011)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Sketch the graph of \( y = (3 - x)^3 \), showing where the curve meets the axes and describing the behaviour at the \( x \)-intercept.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Expand to identify the leading term.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(3-x)^3 = 27 - 27x + 9x^2 - x^3
\end{gathered}\]
The leading term is \( -x^3 \): negative coefficient, so top-left to bottom-right shape.

\textit{Step 2: Find the \( y \)-intercept.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = (3-0)^3 = 27
\end{gathered}\]
The curve crosses the \( y \)-axis at \( 27 \).

\textit{Step 3: Find the \( x \)-intercept.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(3 - x)^3 = 0 \Rightarrow x = 3 \;(\text{triple root})
\end{gathered}\]
A triple root still crosses the \( x \)-axis, but the curve flattens out as it crosses.

\textit{Step 4: Describe the sketch.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Enters top-left, passes through } (0, 27), \text{ flattens and crosses at } x = 3
\end{gathered}\]
The triple root gives an inflection-type crossing, not a sharp cut.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Cubic with triple root at \(\displaystyle x = 3\); \(\displaystyle y\)-intercept at \(\displaystyle (0, 27)\); top-left to bottom-right overall shape.
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}

\section*{Curve Sketching 12\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-012)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 12 (cg5-012)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Sketch the graph of \( y = -x^2(x + 4) \), showing where the curve meets the axes and describing the behaviour at each \( x \)-intercept.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Identify the leading term.}
\[\begin{gathered}
-x^2 \cdot x = -x^3 \quad (\text{negative})
\end{gathered}\]
Top-left to bottom-right overall shape.

\textit{Step 2: Find the \( y \)-intercept.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = -(0)^2(0 + 4) = 0
\end{gathered}\]
The curve passes through the origin.

\textit{Step 3: Find the \( x \)-intercepts.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 = 0 \Rightarrow x = 0 \;(\text{double}); \quad x + 4 = 0 \Rightarrow x = -4 \;(\text{single})
\end{gathered}\]
Touches at \( x = 0 \) and crosses at \( x = -4 \).

\textit{Step 4: Describe the sketch.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Crosses at } x = -4, \text{ touches at } x = 0; \text{ falls to bottom-right}
\end{gathered}\]
The curve enters from top-left, crosses at \( -4 \), rises to touch the origin, then falls away to the right.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Cubic crossing at \(\displaystyle x = -4\), touching at \(\displaystyle x = 0\); passes through origin; top-left to bottom-right shape.
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}

\section*{Curve Sketching 13\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-013)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 13 (cg5-013)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Sketch the graph of \( y = x(x - 5)^2 \), showing all intercepts and describing the nature of each root.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Identify the leading term.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x \cdot x^2 = x^3 \quad (\text{positive})
\end{gathered}\]
Bottom-left to top-right overall.

\textit{Step 2: Find the \( y \)-intercept.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 0 \cdot (0-5)^2 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Passes through the origin.

\textit{Step 3: Find the \( x \)-intercepts.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 0 \;(\text{single root}); \quad (x-5)^2 = 0 \Rightarrow x = 5 \;(\text{double root})
\end{gathered}\]
Crosses at \( x = 0 \), touches at \( x = 5 \).

\textit{Step 4: Describe the sketch.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Crosses at } 0, \text{ rises, touches at } 5 \text{ (bouncing off)}, \text{ then rises away}
\end{gathered}\]
The curve crosses the origin, climbs to a local maximum, dips to touch \( x = 5 \) and rises again.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Cubic crossing at \(\displaystyle x = 0\), touching at \(\displaystyle x = 5\); \(\displaystyle y\)-intercept at origin; positive shape.
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}

\section*{Curve Sketching 14\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-014)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 14 (cg5-014)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{a) Factorise completely \( 2x^3 - 12x^2 + 18x \). \\[3pt] b) Hence sketch the graph, showing where it meets the coordinate axes.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Part a: take out the common factor.}
\[\begin{gathered}
2x^3 - 12x^2 + 18x = 2x(x^2 - 6x + 9)
\end{gathered}\]
Each term has a factor of \( 2x \).

\textit{Step 2: Factorise the quadratic.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 - 6x + 9 = (x - 3)^2
\end{gathered}\]
Perfect square: \( (x-3)^2 = x^2 - 6x + 9 \).

\textit{Step 3: Write the full factorisation.}
\[\begin{gathered}
2x(x - 3)^2
\end{gathered}\]
One single root at \( x = 0 \), one double root at \( x = 3 \).

\textit{Step 4: Part b: find intercepts.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 0 \;(\text{crosses}), \quad x = 3 \;(\text{touches}); \quad y(0) = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Positive leading coefficient: bottom-left to top-right.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad a) \(\displaystyle 2x(x-3)^2\); b) Crosses at \(\displaystyle x = 0\), touches at \(\displaystyle x = 3\); positive cubic shape.
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}

\section*{Curve Sketching 15\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-015)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 15 (cg5-015)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Sketch the graph of \( y = x(x - 1)(x + 1)(x - 3) \), showing where the curve meets the axes.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Identify the degree and leading coefficient.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x \cdot x \cdot x \cdot x = x^4 \quad (\text{positive})
\end{gathered}\]
Even degree, positive leading coefficient: u-shape overall.

\textit{Step 2: Find the \( y \)-intercept.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 0(-1)(1)(-3) = 0
\end{gathered}\]
The curve passes through the origin.

\textit{Step 3: Find the \( x \)-intercepts.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 0,\ -1,\ 1,\ 3
\end{gathered}\]
Four distinct real roots --- the curve crosses the axis four times.

\textit{Step 4: Describe the sketch.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Quartic crossing at } x = -1, 0, 1, 3; \text{ u-shape overall}
\end{gathered}\]
The curve rises from bottom-left, crosses and undulates through all four roots, and rises again to the right.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Quartic crossing at \(\displaystyle x = -1, 0, 1, 3\); \(\displaystyle y\)-intercept at origin; u-shape overall.
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}

\section*{Curve Sketching 16\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-016)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 16 (cg5-016)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Sketch the graph of \( y = -2x(x + 3)(x - 1)(x - 4) \), showing where the curve meets the axes.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Identify the leading term.}
\[\begin{gathered}
-2x \cdot x \cdot x \cdot x = -2x^4 \quad (\text{negative})
\end{gathered}\]
Even degree, negative leading coefficient: n-shape overall (opens downward).

\textit{Step 2: Find the \( y \)-intercept.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = -2(0)(3)(-1)(-4) = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Passes through the origin.

\textit{Step 3: Find the \( x \)-intercepts.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 0,\ -3,\ 1,\ 4
\end{gathered}\]
Four distinct roots.

\textit{Step 4: Describe the sketch.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{n-shape crossing at } x = -3, 0, 1, 4
\end{gathered}\]
The curve falls from top-left, crossing and undulating through the four roots, then falls to the right.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Quartic crossing at \(\displaystyle x = -3, 0, 1, 4\); \(\displaystyle y\)-intercept at origin; n-shape overall.
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}

\section*{Curve Sketching 17\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-017)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 17 (cg5-017)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Sketch the graph of \( y = x^2(x + 2)(x - 3) \), showing all intercepts and the nature of each root.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Identify the leading term.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 \cdot x \cdot x = x^4 \quad (\text{positive})
\end{gathered}\]
u-shape overall.

\textit{Step 2: Find the \( y \)-intercept.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 0^2(2)(-3) = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Passes through the origin.

\textit{Step 3: Find the \( x \)-intercepts and their nature.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 0 \;(\text{double, touches}); \quad x = -2 \;(\text{crosses}); \quad x = 3 \;(\text{crosses})
\end{gathered}\]
The double root at \( x = 0 \) means the curve touches the axis there.

\textit{Step 4: Describe the sketch.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Crosses at } -2 \text{ and } 3, \text{ touches at } 0
\end{gathered}\]
The curve enters from bottom-left, crosses at \( -2 \), touches origin, then crosses at \( 3 \) and rises.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Quartic touching at \(\displaystyle x = 0\), crossing at \(\displaystyle x = -2\) and \(\displaystyle x = 3\); \(\displaystyle y\)-intercept at origin.
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}

\section*{Curve Sketching 18\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-018)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 18 (cg5-018)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Sketch the graph of \( y = (x - 2)^2(x + 1)^2 \), showing all intercepts and describing the behaviour at each.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Identify the leading term.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 \cdot x^2 = x^4 \quad (\text{positive})
\end{gathered}\]
u-shape overall.

\textit{Step 2: Find the \( y \)-intercept.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = (0-2)^2(0+1)^2 = 4 \times 1 = 4
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( x = 0 \).

\textit{Step 3: Find the \( x \)-intercepts.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x-2)^2 = 0 \Rightarrow x = 2 \;(\text{double}); \quad (x+1)^2 = 0 \Rightarrow x = -1 \;(\text{double})
\end{gathered}\]
Both roots are double roots --- the curve touches but does not cross the \( x \)-axis at both.

\textit{Step 4: Describe the sketch.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Touches at } x = -1 \text{ and } x = 2; \; y\text{-intercept at } (0, 4)
\end{gathered}\]
The entire curve sits on or above the \( x \)-axis (since \( y \geq 0 \) for all \( x \)).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Quartic touching \(\displaystyle x\)-axis at \(\displaystyle x = -1\) and \(\displaystyle x = 2\); \(\displaystyle y\)-intercept at \(\displaystyle (0, 4)\); curve lies above \(\displaystyle x\)-axis.
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Curve Sketching 19\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-019)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 19 (cg5-019)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{a) Expand \( (x^2 - 9)(x^2 - 4) \). \\[3pt] b) Hence sketch the graph of \( y = (x^2 - 9)(x^2 - 4) \), showing where it meets the axes.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Part a: factorise each factor as a difference of two squares.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x^2-9)(x^2-4) = (x-3)(x+3)(x-2)(x+2)
\end{gathered}\]
\( x^2 - 9 = (x-3)(x+3) \) and \( x^2 - 4 = (x-2)(x+2) \).

\textit{Step 2: Expand fully (optional, for identification).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^4 - 13x^2 + 36
\end{gathered}\]
Leading term is \( x^4 \) (positive): u-shape overall.

\textit{Step 3: Part b: find the \( x \)-intercepts.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \pm 3,\ \pm 2
\end{gathered}\]
Four distinct roots, symmetric about the \( y \)-axis.

\textit{Step 4: Find the \( y \)-intercept.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = (-9)(-4) = 36
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( x = 0 \) into the original factored form.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Quartic crossing at \(\displaystyle x = \pm 2, \pm 3\); \(\displaystyle y\)-intercept at \(\displaystyle (0, 36)\); u-shape overall; symmetric about the \(\displaystyle y\)-axis.
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}

\section*{Curve Sketching 20\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-020)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 20 (cg5-020)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Sketch the graph of \( y = 3x(x - 2)^2(x + 1) \), showing all intercepts and the nature of each.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Identify the leading term.}
\[\begin{gathered}
3x \cdot x^2 \cdot x = 3x^4 \quad (\text{positive})
\end{gathered}\]
u-shape overall.

\textit{Step 2: Find the \( y \)-intercept.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 3(0)(0-2)^2(0+1) = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Passes through the origin.

\textit{Step 3: Find the \( x \)-intercepts.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 0 \;(\text{crosses}); \quad x = 2 \;(\text{double, touches}); \quad x = -1 \;(\text{crosses})
\end{gathered}\]
Three distinct \( x \)-values; \( x = 2 \) is a double root.

\textit{Step 4: Describe the sketch.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Crosses at } x = -1, 0; \text{ touches at } x = 2
\end{gathered}\]
The curve rises from bottom-left, crosses at \( -1 \) and \( 0 \), bounces off \( x = 2 \), then rises to the right.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Quartic crossing at \(\displaystyle x = -1, 0\), touching at \(\displaystyle x = 2\); \(\displaystyle y\)-intercept at origin; u-shape.
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}

\section*{Curve Sketching 21\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-021)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 21 (cg5-021)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Sketch the graph of \( y = -x^2(x - 4)^2 \), showing all intercepts and the nature of each root.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Identify the leading term.}
\[\begin{gathered}
-x^2 \cdot x^2 = -x^4 \quad (\text{negative})
\end{gathered}\]
Even degree, negative leading coefficient: n-shape (opens downward).

\textit{Step 2: Find the \( y \)-intercept.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = -(0)^2(0-4)^2 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Passes through the origin.

\textit{Step 3: Find the \( x \)-intercepts.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 0 \;(\text{double, touches}); \quad x = 4 \;(\text{double, touches})
\end{gathered}\]
Both roots are double: the curve touches the axis at both but does not cross.

\textit{Step 4: Describe the sketch.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Touches at } x = 0 \text{ and } x = 4; \text{ lies below }x\text{-axis between them}
\end{gathered}\]
The curve is entirely on or below the \( x \)-axis (since \( y \leq 0 \) for all \( x \)).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Quartic touching \(\displaystyle x\)-axis at \(\displaystyle x = 0\) and \(\displaystyle x = 4\); curve lies on or below \(\displaystyle x\)-axis; n-shape.
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}

\section*{Curve Sketching 22\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-022)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 22 (cg5-022)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{a) Factorise \( x^4 - 5x^2 + 4 \) completely. \\[3pt] b) Hence sketch the graph of \( y = x^4 - 5x^2 + 4 \), showing where it meets the axes.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Part a: let \( u = x^2 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
u^2 - 5u + 4 = (u-1)(u-4)
\end{gathered}\]
Factors of 4 that add to \(-5\): \(-1\) and \(-4\).

\textit{Step 2: Replace \( u \) with \( x^2 \) and factorise further.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x^2 - 1)(x^2 - 4) = (x-1)(x+1)(x-2)(x+2)
\end{gathered}\]
Each quadratic factor is a difference of two squares.

\textit{Step 3: Part b: find the \( x \)-intercepts.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \pm 1,\ \pm 2
\end{gathered}\]
Four distinct roots, symmetric about the \( y \)-axis.

\textit{Step 4: Find the \( y \)-intercept.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 0 - 0 + 4 = 4
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( x = 0 \).

\textit{Step 5: Describe the shape.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^4 \text{ has positive coefficient: u-shape overall}
\end{gathered}\]
The curve rises from bottom-left and bottom-right, crossing at \( \pm 1 \) and \( \pm 2 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad a) \(\displaystyle (x-1)(x+1)(x-2)(x+2)\); b) Quartic crossing at \(\displaystyle \pm 1, \pm 2\); \(\displaystyle y\)-intercept \(\displaystyle (0, 4)\); u-shape.
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}

\section*{Curve Sketching 23\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-023)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 23 (cg5-023)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Sketch the graph of \( y = \dfrac{4}{x} \), stating the equations of the asymptotes and the coordinates of the points where \( x = 1 \) and \( x = -1 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Identify the form: \( y = kx^{-1} \) with \( k = 4 > 0 \) and odd power.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 4x^{-1}
\end{gathered}\]
Odd power, positive \( k \): graph in top-right and bottom-left quadrants.

\textit{Step 2: State the asymptotes.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 0 \quad \text{and} \quad y = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Reciprocal functions never touch the axes --- these are the asymptotes.

\textit{Step 3: Find the point where \( x = 1 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = \frac{4}{1} = 4 \implies (1, 4)
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( x = 1 \).

\textit{Step 4: Find the point where \( x = -1 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = \frac{4}{-1} = -4 \implies (-1, -4)
\end{gathered}\]
By symmetry, \( (-1, -4) \) lies on the lower-left branch.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Asymptotes \(\displaystyle x = 0, y = 0\); passes through \(\displaystyle (1, 4)\) and \(\displaystyle (-1, -4)\); two branches in top-right and bottom-left quadrants.
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Curve Sketching 24\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-024)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 24 (cg5-024)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Sketch the graph of \( y = -\dfrac{5}{x} \), stating the asymptotes and the coordinates where \( x = 1 \) and \( x = -1 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Identify: \( y = -5x^{-1} \), odd power, negative \( k \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
k = -5 < 0, \quad n = 1 \text{ (odd)}
\end{gathered}\]
Odd power, negative \( k \): graph in top-left and bottom-right quadrants.

\textit{Step 2: State the asymptotes.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 0 \quad \text{and} \quad y = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Same asymptotes as all basic reciprocal functions.

\textit{Step 3: Point at \( x = 1 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = -\frac{5}{1} = -5 \implies (1, -5)
\end{gathered}\]
This lies in the bottom-right quadrant.

\textit{Step 4: Point at \( x = -1 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = -\frac{5}{-1} = 5 \implies (-1, 5)
\end{gathered}\]
This lies in the top-left quadrant.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Asymptotes \(\displaystyle x = 0, y = 0\); passes through \(\displaystyle (1, -5)\) and \(\displaystyle (-1, 5)\); branches in top-left and bottom-right.
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Curve Sketching 25\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-025)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 25 (cg5-025)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Sketch the graph of \( y = \dfrac{3}{x^2} \), stating the asymptotes and the points where \( x = 1 \) and \( x = -1 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Identify: \( y = 3x^{-2} \), even power, positive \( k \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
k = 3 > 0, \quad n = 2 \text{ (even)}
\end{gathered}\]
Even power, positive \( k \): both branches above the \( x \)-axis.

\textit{Step 2: State the asymptotes.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 0 \quad \text{and} \quad y = 0
\end{gathered}\]
The curve approaches both axes but never touches them.

\textit{Step 3: Points at \( x = \pm 1 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = \frac{3}{1} = 3 \implies (1, 3) \text{ and } (-1, 3)
\end{gathered}\]
Even power means \( x \) and \( -x \) give the same \( y \)-value: the graph is symmetric about the \( y \)-axis.

\textit{Step 4: Describe the sketch.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Two branches, both above }x\text{-axis, symmetric about }y\text{-axis}
\end{gathered}\]
Both branches approach the asymptotes as \( x \to 0 \) and as \( x \to \pm\infty \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Asymptotes \(\displaystyle x = 0, y = 0\); passes through \(\displaystyle (\pm 1, 3)\); both branches above \(\displaystyle x\)-axis; symmetric about \(\displaystyle y\)-axis.
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Curve Sketching 26\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-026)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 26 (cg5-026)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Sketch the graph of \( y = -\dfrac{2}{x^4} \), stating the asymptotes and the points where \( x = 1 \) and \( x = -1 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Identify: \( y = -2x^{-4} \), even power, negative \( k \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
k = -2 < 0, \quad n = 4 \text{ (even)}
\end{gathered}\]
Even power, negative \( k \): both branches below the \( x \)-axis.

\textit{Step 2: State the asymptotes.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 0 \quad \text{and} \quad y = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Standard asymptotes for all reciprocal functions of this type.

\textit{Step 3: Points at \( x = \pm 1 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = -\frac{2}{1^4} = -2 \implies (1, -2) \text{ and } (-1, -2)
\end{gathered}\]
Both points at \( y = -2 \) by symmetry.

\textit{Step 4: Describe the sketch.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Two branches, both below }x\text{-axis, symmetric about }y\text{-axis}
\end{gathered}\]
The entire graph lies below the \( x \)-axis.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Asymptotes \(\displaystyle x = 0, y = 0\); passes through \(\displaystyle (\pm 1, -2)\); both branches below \(\displaystyle x\)-axis.
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}

\small The curve plotted from its equation, with key features marked.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Curve Sketching 27\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-027)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 27 (cg5-027)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Sketch the graphs of \( y = \dfrac{6}{x} \) and \( y = \dfrac{6}{x^2} \) on the same axes, for \( x > 0 \). State one key difference between the two graphs in the first quadrant.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Sketch \( y = \frac{6}{x} \) for \( x > 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 1 \Rightarrow y = 6; \quad x = 2 \Rightarrow y = 3; \quad x = 6 \Rightarrow y = 1
\end{gathered}\]
This is a standard hyperbola branch in the first quadrant.

\textit{Step 2: Sketch \( y = \frac{6}{x^2} \) for \( x > 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 1 \Rightarrow y = 6; \quad x = 2 \Rightarrow y = 1.5; \quad x = 3 \Rightarrow y = \tfrac{2}{3}
\end{gathered}\]
This curve falls more steeply because \( x^2 \) grows faster than \( x \).

\textit{Step 3: Both pass through \( (1, 6) \); compare their rates of decrease.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{6}{x^2} < \frac{6}{x} \text{ for } x > 1
\end{gathered}\]
The \( x^{-2} \) curve decreases more rapidly than the \( x^{-1} \) curve.

\textit{Step 4: State the key difference.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = \frac{6}{x^2} \text{ falls more steeply than } y = \frac{6}{x} \text{ for } x > 1
\end{gathered}\]
Both have the same asymptotes, but the \( 1/x^2 \) curve approaches zero faster.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Both curves pass through \(\displaystyle (1,6)\) and have asymptotes \(\displaystyle x=0, y=0\). The graph of \(\displaystyle y = \frac{6}{x^2}\) decreases more steeply for \(\displaystyle x > 1\).
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}

\section*{Curve Sketching 28\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-028)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 28 (cg5-028)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Match each function below to the correct description of its graph. \\[3pt] Functions: \( y = 5x^{-3} \), \( y = -\dfrac{1}{x^2} \), \( y = \dfrac{7}{x^4} \), \( y = -4x^{-5} \) \\[3pt] Descriptions: - P: both branches above the \( x \)-axis, side by side - Q: top-left and bottom-right quadrant branches - R: top-right and bottom-left quadrant branches - S: both branches below the \( x \)-axis, side by side}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: \( y = 5x^{-3} \): odd power (3), positive \( k \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Odd, positive} \Rightarrow \text{top-right and bottom-left} \Rightarrow R
\end{gathered}\]
Odd power with positive coefficient gives the standard hyperbola orientation.

\textit{Step 2: \( y = -\frac{1}{x^2} \): even power (2), negative \( k \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Even, negative} \Rightarrow \text{both branches below} \Rightarrow S
\end{gathered}\]
Even power means side-by-side branches; negative means below the axis.

\textit{Step 3: \( y = \frac{7}{x^4} \): even power (4), positive \( k \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Even, positive} \Rightarrow \text{both branches above} \Rightarrow P
\end{gathered}\]
Even power means side-by-side; positive means above the axis.

\textit{Step 4: \( y = -4x^{-5} \): odd power (5), negative \( k \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Odd, negative} \Rightarrow \text{top-left and bottom-right} \Rightarrow Q
\end{gathered}\]
Odd power with negative coefficient flips the standard orientation.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle 5x^{-3} \to R\); \(\displaystyle -1/x^2 \to S\); \(\displaystyle 7/x^4 \to P\); \(\displaystyle -4x^{-5} \to Q\)
\end{answerbox}
\medskip\hrule

\section*{Curve Sketching 29\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-029)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 29 (cg5-029)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{a) Sketch the graphs of \( y = \dfrac{2}{x^2} \) and \( y = -x^2 + 3 \) on the same axes. \\[3pt] b) Hence state the number of real solutions to \( \dfrac{2}{x^2} = -x^2 + 3 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Sketch \( y = \frac{2}{x^2} \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Even power, positive }k: \text{ both branches above }x\text{-axis; asymptotes } x=0, y=0
\end{gathered}\]
Passes through \( (1, 2) \) and \( (-1, 2) \).

\textit{Step 2: Sketch \( y = -x^2 + 3 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Downward parabola; vertex at } (0, 3); \text{ crosses }x\text{-axis at } x = \pm\sqrt{3}
\end{gathered}\]
This is an inverted parabola with \( y \)-intercept 3.

\textit{Step 3: Solve the equation algebraically to confirm the count.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\tfrac{2}{x^2} = -x^2 + 3 \implies 2 = -x^4 + 3x^2 \implies x^4 - 3x^2 + 2 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Multiply both sides by \( x^2 \) (valid since \( x \neq 0 \) --- the reciprocal curve excludes this) and rearrange. This is a quadratic in \( x^2 \).

\textit{Step 4: Factorise and solve.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x^2 - 1)(x^2 - 2) = 0 \implies x = \pm 1,\ \pm\sqrt{2}
\end{gathered}\]
Each value of \( x^2 \) gives two real \( x \)-values, so there are four real solutions. Graphically, the parabola and the reciprocal curve cross twice on each side of the \( y \)-axis.

\textit{Step 5: State the conclusion.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\Rightarrow 4 \text{ real solutions}
\end{gathered}\]
Each intersection of the graphs corresponds to one real solution of the equation.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad The equation has \(\displaystyle 4\) real solutions: \(\displaystyle x = \pm 1, \pm\sqrt{2}\).
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Curve Sketching 30\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-030)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 30 (cg5-030)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Sketch the graph of \( y = (4 - x)(x + 1)^2 \), showing all intercepts and describing the behaviour at each root.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Determine the leading term.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(4-x)(x+1)^2 \approx (-x)(x^2) = -x^3 \quad (\text{for large }|x|)
\end{gathered}\]
Negative leading coefficient: top-left to bottom-right shape.

\textit{Step 2: Find the \( y \)-intercept.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = (4)(1)^2 = 4
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( x = 0 \).

\textit{Step 3: Find the \( x \)-intercepts and their nature.}
\[\begin{gathered}
4 - x = 0 \Rightarrow x = 4 \;(\text{crosses}); \quad (x+1)^2 = 0 \Rightarrow x = -1 \;(\text{double, touches})
\end{gathered}\]
Single root at \( x = 4 \), double root at \( x = -1 \).

\textit{Step 4: Describe the sketch.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Enters top-left, touches } (-1,0), \text{ passes through } (0,4), \text{ crosses } (4,0), \text{ falls bottom-right}
\end{gathered}\]
The curve bounces off the axis at \( x = -1 \) and cuts through at \( x = 4 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Cubic touching at \(\displaystyle x = -1\), crossing at \(\displaystyle x = 4\); \(\displaystyle y\)-intercept at \(\displaystyle (0, 4)\); top-left to bottom-right shape.
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}

\section*{Curve Sketching 31\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-031)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 31 (cg5-031)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Sketch the graph of \( y = x^4 - 16 \), showing where it meets the coordinate axes.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Identify the shape.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^4 \text{ with positive coefficient: u-shape}
\end{gathered}\]
Even degree, positive leading coefficient.

\textit{Step 2: Find the \( y \)-intercept.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 0 - 16 = -16
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( x = 0 \).

\textit{Step 3: Find the \( x \)-intercepts by factorising.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^4 - 16 = (x^2 - 4)(x^2 + 4) = (x-2)(x+2)(x^2+4)
\end{gathered}\]
\( x^2 + 4 = 0 \) has no real solutions, so only real roots are \( x = \pm 2 \).

\textit{Step 4: Describe the sketch.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Crosses } x\text{-axis at } \pm 2; \; y\text{-intercept at } (0,-16)
\end{gathered}\]
The curve crosses the \( x \)-axis at two points, dips to \( -16 \) at the origin.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Quartic crossing at \(\displaystyle x = \pm 2\); \(\displaystyle y\)-intercept at \(\displaystyle (0, -16)\); u-shape.
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}

\section*{Curve Sketching 32\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-032)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 32 (cg5-032)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 7}

\question{a) Factorise completely \( y = x^3 - 4x^2 + 4x \). \\[3pt] b) Sketch the graph, showing all intercepts and describing the nature of each root. \\[3pt] c) Write down the number of distinct real roots of \( x^3 - 4x^2 + 4x = 0 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Part a: take out the common factor \( x \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^3 - 4x^2 + 4x = x(x^2 - 4x + 4)
\end{gathered}\]
Every term contains \( x \).

\textit{Step 2: Factorise the quadratic.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 - 4x + 4 = (x - 2)^2
\end{gathered}\]
Perfect square trinomial: \( (x-2)^2 = x^2 - 4x + 4 \).

\textit{Step 3: Write the full factorisation.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x(x-2)^2
\end{gathered}\]
Single root at \( x = 0 \), double root at \( x = 2 \).

\textit{Step 4: Part b: find intercepts.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 0 \;(\text{crosses}); \quad x = 2 \;(\text{double, touches}); \quad y(0) = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Positive cubic shape (bottom-left to top-right).

\textit{Step 5: Part c: count distinct real roots.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 0 \text{ (once)}, \quad x = 2 \text{ (once, even though doubled)}
\end{gathered}\]
There are 2 distinct real roots: \( x = 0 \) and \( x = 2 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad a) \(\displaystyle x(x-2)^2\); b) Crosses at \(\displaystyle x = 0\), touches at \(\displaystyle x = 2\); c) 2 distinct real roots.
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}

\section*{Curve Sketching 33\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-033)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 33 (cg5-033)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{The graph of \( y = f(x) \) is a quartic with positive leading coefficient. It crosses the \( x \)-axis at \( x = -2 \), touches the \( x \)-axis at \( x = 1 \), and crosses again at \( x = 4 \). \\[3pt] a) Write down a possible equation for \( f(x) \) in factorised form. \\[3pt] b) Find the \( y \)-intercept of this graph.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Part a: use the root information to write the factors.}
\[\begin{gathered}
f(x) = (x + 2)(x - 1)^2(x - 4)
\end{gathered}\]
Cross at \( -2 \) and \( 4 \) gives single factors; touch at \( 1 \) gives a squared factor. The product of four linear factors gives degree 4 with positive leading coefficient.

\textit{Step 2: Check the leading coefficient.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x \cdot x^2 \cdot x = x^4 \quad (\text{positive})
\end{gathered}\]
Confirmed: positive leading coefficient.

\textit{Step 3: Part b: find the \( y \)-intercept.}
\[\begin{gathered}
f(0) = (0+2)(0-1)^2(0-4) = 2 \times 1 \times (-4) = -8
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( x = 0 \) into the factorised equation.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad a) \(\displaystyle f(x) = (x+2)(x-1)^2(x-4)\); b) \(\displaystyle y\)-intercept at \(\displaystyle (0, -8)\).
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}

\section*{Curve Sketching 34\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-034)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 34 (cg5-034)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{a) On the same axes, sketch the graphs of \( y = \dfrac{1}{x^3} \) and \( y = x^3 \). \\[3pt] b) State the number of real solutions to \( \dfrac{1}{x^3} = x^3 \), and find them.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Part a: sketch \( y = x^3 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Odd power, positive: bottom-left to top-right; passes through origin}
\end{gathered}\]
Standard cubic shape through \( (-1,-1), (0,0), (1,1) \).

\textit{Step 2: Sketch \( y = \frac{1}{x^3} = x^{-3} \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Odd power, positive: top-right and bottom-left quadrants; asymptotes } x=0, y=0
\end{gathered}\]
Passes through \( (1,1) \) and \( (-1,-1) \).

\textit{Step 3: Part b: find intersections algebraically.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{1}{x^3} = x^3 \implies x^6 = 1 \implies x = \pm 1
\end{gathered}\]
Multiply both sides by \( x^3 \) (note \( x \neq 0 \)), then solve \( x^6 = 1 \).

\textit{Step 4: Check: \( x = 1 \) gives \( y = 1 \); \( x = -1 \) gives \( y = -1 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Intersections at } (1, 1) \text{ and } (-1, -1)
\end{gathered}\]
Both solutions confirmed.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad b) 2 real solutions: \(\displaystyle x = 1\) and \(\displaystyle x = -1\).
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}

\section*{Curve Sketching 35\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-035)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 35 (cg5-035)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 8}

\question{The graph of \( y = x^4 - 10x^3 + 35x^2 - 50x + 24 \) has four positive integer roots. \\[3pt] a) By trying small positive integers, find all four roots. \\[3pt] b) Write the function in fully factorised form. \\[3pt] c) Sketch the graph, showing all intercepts.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Part a: try \( x = 1 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
1 - 10 + 35 - 50 + 24 = 0 \checkmark
\end{gathered}\]
\( x = 1 \) is a root.

\textit{Step 2: Try \( x = 2 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
16 - 80 + 140 - 100 + 24 = 0 \checkmark
\end{gathered}\]
\( x = 2 \) is a root.

\textit{Step 3: Try \( x = 3 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
81 - 270 + 315 - 150 + 24 = 0 \checkmark
\end{gathered}\]
\( x = 3 \) is a root.

\textit{Step 4: By Vieta's formulas (sum of roots = 10), find the fourth root.}
\[\begin{gathered}
1 + 2 + 3 + r_4 = 10 \implies r_4 = 4
\end{gathered}\]
The sum of the roots of a monic quartic equals the negative of the \( x^3 \) coefficient: \( -(-10) = 10 \).

\textit{Step 5: Part b: write the factorised form.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = (x-1)(x-2)(x-3)(x-4)
\end{gathered}\]
Verify by noting the product of roots \( = 1 \times 2 \times 3 \times 4 = 24 \) matches the constant term. \( \checkmark \)

\textit{Step 6: Part c: find the \( y \)-intercept and describe the sketch.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y(0) = (-1)(-2)(-3)(-4) = 24
\end{gathered}\]
Positive leading coefficient (\( x^4 \)), u-shape overall. Crosses at \( x = 1, 2, 3, 4 \); \( y \)-intercept at \( 24 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad a) Roots: \(\displaystyle 1, 2, 3, 4\); b) \(\displaystyle y = (x-1)(x-2)(x-3)(x-4)\); c) Quartic crossing at \(\displaystyle x = 1,2,3,4\); \(\displaystyle y\)-intercept at \(\displaystyle (0, 24)\); u-shape.
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}

\section*{Curve Sketching 36\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-036)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 36 (cg5-036)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Sketch \( y = x^2 - 4 \), showing the intercepts with both axes.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: y-intercept: set x = 0}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = -4
\end{gathered}\]
Substituting \( x = 0 \) into \( y = x^2 - 4 \) gives the \( y \)-axis crossing.

\textit{Step 2: x-intercepts: set y = 0}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 = 4 \implies x = \pm 2
\end{gathered}\]
Take square roots of both sides --- remember the \( \pm \) because both \( 2 \) and \( -2 \) square to \( 4 \).

\textit{Step 3: Shape}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{U-shape, vertex at } (0, -4)
\end{gathered}\]
Positive coefficient of \( x^2 \) gives a u-shape; the curve \( y = x^2 - 4 \) is \( y = x^2 \) shifted down by 4, so the vertex moves from \( (0,0) \) to \( (0,-4) \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad U-shape; roots at \(\displaystyle (-2,0)\) and \(\displaystyle (2,0)\); \(\displaystyle y\)-intercept \(\displaystyle (0,-4)\)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}

\section*{Curve Sketching 37\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-037)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 37 (cg5-037)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Sketch \( y = (x-1)(x+3) \), showing all intercepts and the vertex.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: x-intercepts}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 1 \text{ and } x = -3
\end{gathered}\]
Setting \( y = 0 \), each factor must equal zero, giving the two roots directly from the factorised form.

\textit{Step 2: y-intercept}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = (-1)(3) = -3
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( x = 0 \) into the factorised form: \( (0-1)(0+3) = (-1)(3) \).

\textit{Step 3: Vertex (midpoint of roots)}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \frac{1+(-3)}{2} = -1; \quad y = (-2)(2) = -4
\end{gathered}\]
A parabola is symmetric, so its vertex sits at the midpoint of the two roots. Substituting \( x = -1 \) back into the factored form gives the \( y \)-coordinate.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad U-shape; roots \(\displaystyle (1,0)\), \(\displaystyle (-3,0)\); vertex \(\displaystyle (-1,-4)\); \(\displaystyle y\)-int \(\displaystyle (0,-3)\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Curve Sketching 38\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-038)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 38 (cg5-038)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Sketch \( y = -x^2 + 6x - 5 \), showing the vertex and all intercepts.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Factorise}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = -(x-1)(x-5)
\end{gathered}\]
Factor out \(-1\) to get \( -(x^2 - 6x + 5) \), then factorise the bracket: numbers multiplying to \(5\) and adding to \(-6\) are \(-1\) and \(-5\).

\textit{Step 2: Vertex}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 3; \quad y = -9 + 18 - 5 = 4
\end{gathered}\]
Negative coefficient of \( x^2 \) means the parabola opens downwards, so the vertex is the maximum. It sits on the axis of symmetry midway between the roots: \( x = \tfrac{1+5}{2} = 3 \).

\textit{Step 3: y-intercept}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = -5
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( x = 0 \) into \( y = -x^2 + 6x - 5 \) to get the constant term \(-5\).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad n-shape; roots \(\displaystyle (1,0)\), \(\displaystyle (5,0)\); vertex \(\displaystyle (3,4)\); \(\displaystyle y\)-int \(\displaystyle (0,-5)\)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Curve Sketching 39\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-039)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 39 (cg5-039)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Sketch \( y = \frac{1}{x} \) for \( x > 0 \) and \( x < 0 \), showing the asymptotes.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Asymptotes}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 0 \text{ (vertical)}, \quad y = 0 \text{ (horizontal)}
\end{gathered}\]
The function is undefined at \( x = 0 \) (vertical asymptote), and as \( |x| \to \infty \) the value \( \tfrac{1}{x} \to 0 \), giving the horizontal asymptote.

\textit{Step 2: Behaviour}
\[\begin{gathered}
x > 0: \text{curve in 1st quadrant}; \quad x < 0: \text{curve in 3rd quadrant}
\end{gathered}\]
Positive \( x \) gives positive \( y \) and negative \( x \) gives negative \( y \), so the two branches lie in opposite (diagonal) quadrants.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Rectangular hyperbola with asymptotes \(\displaystyle x = 0\) and \(\displaystyle y = 0\)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}

\section*{Curve Sketching 41\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-041)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 41 (cg5-041)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Sketch \( y = x^3 - 3x \), showing all stationary points and intercepts.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Intercepts: y = x(x\textasciicircum 2-3)}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 0, \pm\sqrt{3}
\end{gathered}\]
Factor out \( x \) then set each factor to zero. The quadratic \( x^2 - 3 = 0 \) gives \( x = \pm\sqrt{3} \).

\textit{Step 2: Stationary points: dy/dx = 3x\textasciicircum 2 - 3 = 0}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \pm 1; \quad (1,-2) \text{ and } (-1, 2)
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate and set \( \tfrac{dy}{dx} = 0 \). Substitute \( x = 1 \) into \( y = x^3 - 3x \) to get \( y = -2 \) (minimum), and \( x = -1 \) to get \( y = 2 \) (maximum). Positive cubic, so the left turning point is the maximum.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Cubic; roots at \(\displaystyle 0, \pm\sqrt{3}\); max \(\displaystyle (-1,2)\); min \(\displaystyle (1,-2)\)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}

\section*{Curve Sketching 42\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-042)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 42 (cg5-042)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Sketch \( y = \frac{1}{x-2} \), clearly showing the asymptotes.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Asymptotes}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 2 \text{ (vertical)}, \quad y = 0 \text{ (horizontal)}
\end{gathered}\]
This is \( y = \tfrac{1}{x} \) shifted 2 units right (replacing \( x \) with \( x - 2 \)), so the vertical asymptote moves from \( x = 0 \) to \( x = 2 \). The horizontal asymptote \( y = 0 \) is unchanged.

\textit{Step 2: y-intercept}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = \frac{1}{-2} = -\frac{1}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( x = 0 \) into \( \tfrac{1}{x - 2} \) to get the crossing on the \( y \)-axis.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle Translated reciprocal; asymptotes x = 2, y = 0; y-int (0, -1/2)\)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}

\section*{Curve Sketching 43\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-043)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 43 (cg5-043)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Sketch \( y = (x-2)^2(x+1) \), showing all intercepts.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: x-intercepts}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 2 \text{ (repeated)}, \quad x = -1
\end{gathered}\]
Setting each factor to zero: \( (x-2)^2 = 0 \) gives a double root \( x = 2 \) (so the curve touches the axis there), and \( x + 1 = 0 \) gives a single root \( x = -1 \) (the curve crosses).

\textit{Step 2: y-intercept}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = (0-2)^2(0+1) = 4 \times 1 = 4
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( x = 0 \) into each factor: \( (0-2)^2 = 4 \) and \( (0+1) = 1 \), giving \( y = 4 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Cubic; touches \(\displaystyle x\)-axis at \(\displaystyle (2,0)\); crosses at \(\displaystyle (-1,0)\); \(\displaystyle y\)-int \(\displaystyle (0,4)\)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}

\section*{Curve Sketching 44\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-044)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 44 (cg5-044)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Sketch \( y = \sqrt{x} \) for \( x \geq 0 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Key features}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Starts at origin, increases, domain } x \geq 0
\end{gathered}\]
Square roots are only defined for non-negative inputs, so the domain is \( x \geq 0 \). The curve starts at \( (0,0) \), passes through \( (1,1) \) and \( (4,2) \), and gets less steep as \( x \) grows (concave, gradient \( \tfrac{1}{2\sqrt{x}} \) decreases).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Starts at \(\displaystyle (0,0)\), increases and flattens; defined for \(\displaystyle x \geq 0\)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}

\section*{Curve Sketching 45\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-045)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 45 (cg5-045)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Sketch \( y = 2^x \), showing the asymptote and y-intercept.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: y-intercept}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 2^0 = 1
\end{gathered}\]
Any non-zero base raised to the power 0 is 1, so every exponential curve \( y = a^x \) passes through \( (0, 1) \).

\textit{Step 2: Asymptote}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 0 \text{ (horizontal, as } x \to -\infty)
\end{gathered}\]
As \( x \to -\infty \), \( 2^x \to 0 \) but never reaches it, so \( y = 0 \) is a horizontal asymptote. As \( x \to +\infty \), \( 2^x \) grows without bound.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Exponential curve; \(\displaystyle y\)-int \(\displaystyle (0,1)\); asymptote \(\displaystyle y = 0\); increases rapidly
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}

\section*{Curve Sketching 46\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-046)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 46 (cg5-046)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Sketch \( y = x^2 + 2x + 5 \), explaining why it does not cross the x-axis.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Complete the square}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = (x+1)^2 + 4
\end{gathered}\]
Half the coefficient of \( x \) is \( 1 \), so \( x^2 + 2x = (x+1)^2 - 1 \); adding 5 gives \( (x+1)^2 + 4 \). Since \( (x+1)^2 \geq 0 \) always, the minimum value of \( y \) is \( 4 \), reached at \( x = -1 \).

\textit{Step 2: Discriminant}
\[\begin{gathered}
\Delta = 4 - 20 = -16 < 0
\end{gathered}\]
Using \( b^2 - 4ac \) with \( a=1, b=2, c=5 \). A negative discriminant means no real roots, confirming the curve sits entirely above the \( x \)-axis.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad U-shape; vertex \(\displaystyle (-1, 4)\); \(\displaystyle y\)-int \(\displaystyle (0,5)\); no \(\displaystyle x\)-intercepts
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}

\section*{Curve Sketching 47\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-047)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 47 (cg5-047)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Sketch \( y = |x - 3| \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{V-shape with vertex at x = 3}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Vertex at } (3, 0); \quad y\text{-int: } |0-3| = 3
\end{gathered}\]
The modulus reflects any negative output to positive, so \( y = |x-3| \) is \( y = x - 3 \) for \( x \geq 3 \) and \( y = -(x-3) \) for \( x < 3 \). The vertex sits where the inside equals zero (\( x = 3 \)), giving a V-shape with gradient \( \pm 1 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad V-shape; vertex \(\displaystyle (3,0)\); \(\displaystyle y\)-int \(\displaystyle (0,3)\); symmetric about \(\displaystyle x = 3\)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}

\section*{Curve Sketching 48\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-048)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 48 (cg5-048)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Sketch \( y = x^4 - 4x^2 \), showing all intercepts and stationary points.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Factorise}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = x^2(x^2-4) = x^2(x-2)(x+2)
\end{gathered}\]
Take out the common factor \( x^2 \), then apply the difference of two squares to \( x^2 - 4 \). The double root at \( x = 0 \) means the curve touches the axis there, while \( x = \pm 2 \) are single crossings.

\textit{Step 2: Stationary points: dy/dx = 4x\textasciicircum 3 - 8x = 4x(x\textasciicircum 2-2) = 0}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 0, \pm\sqrt{2}; \quad y(0) = 0, \quad y(\pm\sqrt{2}) = 4-8 = -4
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiating and factoring gives three stationary points. At \( x = \pm\sqrt{2} \): \( y = (\sqrt 2)^4 - 4(\sqrt 2)^2 = 4 - 8 = -4 \). Since the leading coefficient is positive (u-shape overall), the outer turning points are minima and the central one at the origin is a local maximum, giving a W-shape.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad W-shape; roots at \(\displaystyle 0, \pm 2\); local max \(\displaystyle (0,0)\); local min \(\displaystyle (\pm\sqrt{2}, -4)\)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}

\section*{Curve Sketching 49\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-049)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 49 (cg5-049)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Sketch \( y = \frac{2x}{x+1} \), showing the asymptotes and intercepts.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Asymptotes}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = -1 \text{ (vertical)}; \quad y = 2 \text{ (horizontal, since } \frac{2x}{x} \to 2)
\end{gathered}\]
The denominator is zero when \( x = -1 \), giving the vertical asymptote. As \( |x| \to \infty \), the lower-order terms become negligible and the ratio tends to \( \tfrac{2x}{x} = 2 \).

\textit{Step 2: Intercepts}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 0: y = 0; \quad y = 0: x = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Substituting \( x = 0 \) gives \( y = 0 \); setting \( y = 0 \) requires \( 2x = 0 \), so \( x = 0 \). Both intercepts coincide at the origin.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Through origin; asymptotes \(\displaystyle x = -1\) and \(\displaystyle y = 2\)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}

\section*{Curve Sketching 50\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-050)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 50 (cg5-050)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{The graph of \( y = f(x) \) passes through \( (0, 3) \) and has a maximum at \( (2, 7) \). Sketch \( y = f(x) + 2 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Vertical translation up 2}
\[\begin{gathered}
(0, 3) \to (0, 5); \quad (2, 7) \to (2, 9)
\end{gathered}\]
Replacing \( f(x) \) with \( f(x) + 2 \) shifts the entire graph upwards by 2 units, so every point keeps its \( x \)-coordinate and has 2 added to its \( y \)-coordinate. The shape and the location of the maximum (relative to the curve) are unchanged.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Same shape, shifted up 2; passes through \(\displaystyle (0,5)\); max at \(\displaystyle (2,9)\)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}

\section*{Curve Sketching 51\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-051)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 51 (cg5-051)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Sketch \( y = (x+2)^3 \), stating the point of inflection.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Translation of y = x\textasciicircum 3}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Point of inflection at } (-2, 0)
\end{gathered}\]
Replacing \( x \) with \( x + 2 \) shifts the graph 2 units to the left. The inflection point of \( y = x^3 \) at the origin therefore moves to \( (-2, 0) \), and this is also a triple root (the curve flattens then crosses the \( x \)-axis).

\textit{Step 2: y-intercept}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 8
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( x = 0 \): \( (0+2)^3 = 8 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Cubic S-shape; inflection \(\displaystyle (-2,0)\); \(\displaystyle y\)-int \(\displaystyle (0,8)\)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Curve Sketching 52\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-052)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 52 (cg5-052)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Sketch \( y = -\frac{1}{x} \), showing the asymptotes.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Reflection of y = 1/x in x-axis}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Asymptotes: } x = 0, y = 0; \text{ curves in 2nd and 4th quadrants}
\end{gathered}\]
Multiplying by \(-1\) reflects \( y = \tfrac{1}{x} \) in the \( x \)-axis, flipping each branch. Positive \( x \) now gives negative \( y \) (4th quadrant) and negative \( x \) gives positive \( y \) (2nd quadrant). The asymptotes \( x = 0 \) and \( y = 0 \) are unchanged.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Hyperbola in 2nd and 4th quadrants; asymptotes \(\displaystyle x = 0, y = 0\)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}

\section*{Curve Sketching 53\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-053)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 53 (cg5-053)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Sketch \( y = x^2 - 6x + 9 \), identifying the nature of the root.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Factorise}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = (x-3)^2
\end{gathered}\]
This is a perfect square trinomial: \( (x-3)^2 = x^2 - 6x + 9 \). The repeated factor means \( x = 3 \) is a double root.

\textit{Step 2: Key features}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Touches x-axis at } (3,0); \quad y\text{-int } (0,9)
\end{gathered}\]
A double root means the curve touches but does not cross the \( x \)-axis at \( x = 3 \). The vertex coincides with this touch point, and substituting \( x = 0 \) gives \( y = 9 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad U-shape; touches \(\displaystyle x\)-axis at \(\displaystyle (3,0)\); vertex \(\displaystyle (3,0)\); \(\displaystyle y\)-int \(\displaystyle (0,9)\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Curve Sketching 54\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-054)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 54 (cg5-054)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Sketch \( y = \frac{1}{x^2} \), showing the asymptotes.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Key features}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Always positive; asymptotes } x = 0 \text{ and } y = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Since \( x^2 > 0 \) for all \( x \neq 0 \), the output \( \tfrac{1}{x^2} \) is always positive --- both branches sit above the \( x \)-axis. Because \( (-x)^2 = x^2 \), the graph is symmetric about the \( y \)-axis, and the asymptotes are the two axes.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Both branches in 1st and 2nd quadrants; asymptotes \(\displaystyle x = 0, y = 0\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Curve Sketching 55\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-055)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 55 (cg5-055)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Given \( y = f(x) \) with roots at -1, 2, 5 and y-intercept 10, sketch \( y = f(x+1) \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Shift left by 1}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{New roots: } -2,\ 1,\ 4; \quad \text{new }y\text{-intercept } = f(1)
\end{gathered}\]
Replacing \( x \) with \( x + 1 \) shifts the graph 1 unit to the left, so each root drops by 1: \( -1 \to -2 \), \( 2 \to 1 \), \( 5 \to 4 \). The new \( y \)-intercept is the value of the original function at \( x = 1 \) (unknown from the data given --- only that \( f(0) = 10 \)).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Same shape shifted 1 left; new roots at \(\displaystyle -2, 1, 4\); new \(\displaystyle y\)-intercept \(\displaystyle = f(1)\) (not determined by data given)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}

\section*{Curve Sketching 56\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-056)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 56 (cg5-056)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Sketch \( y = 3 - x^2 \), showing the vertex and intercepts.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Key features}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{n-shape; vertex } (0,3); \quad x = \pm\sqrt{3}
\end{gathered}\]
Rewriting as \( y = -x^2 + 3 \): the negative leading coefficient gives an n-shape (opens downward), and the constant \(+3\) shifts \( y = -x^2 \) up by 3 so the maximum sits at \( (0,3) \). Setting \( y = 0 \) gives \( x^2 = 3 \Rightarrow x = \pm\sqrt{3} \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad n-shape; vertex \(\displaystyle (0,3)\); roots \(\displaystyle (\pm\sqrt{3}, 0)\); \(\displaystyle y\)-int \(\displaystyle (0,3)\)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}

\section*{Curve Sketching 57\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-057)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 57 (cg5-057)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Sketch \( y = x(x-2)(x+3) \), showing all intercepts.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: x-intercepts}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 0, 2, -3
\end{gathered}\]
Set each factor of the factorised cubic to zero. Three distinct roots mean the curve crosses the \( x \)-axis three times.

\textit{Step 2: y-intercept}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Substituting \( x = 0 \) into \( x(x-2)(x+3) \) gives \( 0 \), so the curve passes through the origin.

\textit{Step 3: End behaviour}
\[\begin{gathered}
x \to +\infty: y \to +\infty; \quad x \to -\infty: y \to -\infty
\end{gathered}\]
Expanding gives leading term \( +x^3 \). Positive coefficient of an odd power means bottom-left to top-right overall shape.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Cubic through \(\displaystyle (-3,0)\), \(\displaystyle (0,0)\), \(\displaystyle (2,0)\); positive leading coefficient
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}

\section*{Curve Sketching 58\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-058)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 58 (cg5-058)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Sketch \( y = \frac{x+1}{x-1} \), showing the asymptotes and intercepts.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Asymptotes}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 1 \text{ (vertical)}; \quad y = 1 \text{ (horizontal)}
\end{gathered}\]
The denominator is zero at \( x = 1 \), giving a vertical asymptote. For large \( |x| \) the ratio behaves like \( \tfrac{x}{x} = 1 \), giving the horizontal asymptote \( y = 1 \).

\textit{Step 2: Intercepts}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 0: y = -1; \quad y = 0: x = -1
\end{gathered}\]
At \( x = 0 \): \( y = \tfrac{0+1}{0-1} = -1 \). Setting the numerator equal to zero (the only way the fraction is zero) gives \( x + 1 = 0 \Rightarrow x = -1 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Asymptotes \(\displaystyle x = 1, y = 1\); intercepts \(\displaystyle (-1,0)\), \(\displaystyle (0,-1)\)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}

\small The curve plotted from its equation, with key features marked.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Curve Sketching 59\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-059)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 59 (cg5-059)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{The curve \( y = f(x) \) has a minimum at \( (1, -3) \) and passes through \( (0, 0) \). Sketch \( y = -f(x) \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Reflect in x-axis}
\[\begin{gathered}
(1, -3) \to (1, 3); \quad (0, 0) \to (0, 0)
\end{gathered}\]
The transformation \( y = -f(x) \) multiplies every \( y \)-value by \(-1\), which reflects the curve in the \( x \)-axis. \( x \)-coordinates stay the same, so the minimum at \( (1, -3) \) becomes a maximum at \( (1, 3) \), and the origin (where \( y = 0 \)) maps to itself.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Reflected curve; max at \(\displaystyle (1,3)\); through origin
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}

\section*{Curve Sketching 60\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-060)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 60 (cg5-060)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Sketch \( y = e^{-x} \), showing the asymptote and intercept.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Key features}
\[\begin{gathered}
y\text{-int: } (0,1); \quad \text{asymptote } y = 0; \quad \text{decreasing}
\end{gathered}\]
\( y = e^{-x} \) is \( y = e^x \) reflected in the \( y \)-axis (because \( x \) is replaced by \( -x \)), so it decreases instead of increases. At \( x = 0 \), \( y = 1 \); as \( x \to +\infty \), \( e^{-x} \to 0 \) but never reaches it, giving the asymptote \( y = 0 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Decreasing exponential; \(\displaystyle y\)-int \(\displaystyle (0,1)\); asymptote \(\displaystyle y = 0\)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}

\section*{Curve Sketching 61\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-061)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 61 (cg5-061)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Sketch \( y = x^2(x-3) \), showing where the curve touches or crosses the x-axis.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Roots}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 0 \text{ (repeated - touches)}, \quad x = 3 \text{ (crosses)}
\end{gathered}\]
Setting \( y = 0 \): the factor \( x^2 \) gives a double root at \( x = 0 \) (curve touches but does not cross), and \( x - 3 = 0 \) gives a single root \( x = 3 \) (curve cuts through).

\textit{Step 2: y-intercept}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Substituting \( x = 0 \) gives \( 0^2 \cdot (-3) = 0 \), so the curve passes through the origin.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Cubic; touches \(\displaystyle x\)-axis at origin, crosses at \(\displaystyle (3,0)\)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}

\section*{Curve Sketching 62\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-062)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 62 (cg5-062)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Sketch \( y = \frac{4}{x+2} + 1 \), showing the asymptotes and intercepts.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Asymptotes}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = -2 \text{ (vertical)}; \quad y = 1 \text{ (horizontal)}
\end{gathered}\]
This is \( y = \tfrac{4}{x} \) shifted 2 left (replace \( x \) with \( x + 2 \)) and 1 up. Each shift carries its asymptote: vertical \( x = 0 \) moves to \( x = -2 \), horizontal \( y = 0 \) moves to \( y = 1 \).

\textit{Step 2: y-intercept}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = \frac{4}{2} + 1 = 3
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( x = 0 \) into \( \tfrac{4}{x+2} + 1 \).

\textit{Step 3: x-intercept}
\[\begin{gathered}
0 = \frac{4}{x+2} + 1 \implies x = -6
\end{gathered}\]
Rearrange: \( \tfrac{4}{x+2} = -1 \), then multiply both sides by \( x + 2 \) to get \( 4 = -(x+2) \), so \( x = -6 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Translated reciprocal; asymptotes \(\displaystyle x = -2, y = 1\); intercepts \(\displaystyle (-6,0)\), \(\displaystyle (0,3)\)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}

\section*{Curve Sketching 63\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-063)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 63 (cg5-063)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Given \( y = f(x) \) with a maximum at \( (3, 5) \), sketch \( y = f(2x) \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Horizontal compression by factor 2}
\[\begin{gathered}
(3, 5) \to (\tfrac{3}{2}, 5)
\end{gathered}\]
The transformation \( y = f(2x) \) replaces \( x \) with \( 2x \), which compresses the graph horizontally by a factor of 2 (each feature happens at half the \( x \)-value). \( y \)-coordinates are unchanged, so the maximum at \( (3, 5) \) becomes a maximum at \( (\tfrac{3}{2}, 5) \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Same height, compressed horizontally; max at \(\displaystyle (3/2, 5)\)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}

\section*{Curve Sketching 64\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-064)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 64 (cg5-064)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Sketch \( y = |2x - 4| \), showing the vertex and intercepts.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Vertex where 2x - 4 = 0}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 2; \quad \text{vertex } (2, 0)
\end{gathered}\]
The modulus function has its corner where the expression inside equals zero. Setting \( 2x - 4 = 0 \) gives \( x = 2 \), and at that point \( y = 0 \).

\textit{Step 2: y-intercept}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = |0-4| = 4
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( x = 0 \): \( |-4| = 4 \). Either side of the vertex the graph is a straight line with gradient \( \pm 2 \), giving the V-shape.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad V-shape; vertex \(\displaystyle (2,0)\); \(\displaystyle y\)-int \(\displaystyle (0,4)\); gradient \(\displaystyle \pm 2\)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}
