\section*{Curve Sketching 65\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-065)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 65 (cg5-065)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Sketch \( y = -x^3 + 3x \), showing the stationary points and intercepts.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Intercepts}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = -x(x^2-3); \quad x = 0, \pm\sqrt{3}
\end{gathered}\]
Factor out \(-x\), then \( x^2 - 3 = 0 \) gives \( x = \pm\sqrt{3} \). Three distinct roots, with the curve passing through the origin.

\textit{Step 2: Stationary points}
\[\begin{gathered}
dy/dx = -3x^2 + 3 = 0 \implies x = \pm 1
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate and set equal to zero. \( 3x^2 = 3 \Rightarrow x^2 = 1 \), so \( x = \pm 1 \).

\textit{Step 3: Values}
\[\begin{gathered}
(-1, -2) \text{ min}; \quad (1, 2) \text{ max}
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute back: \( y(1) = -1 + 3 = 2 \) and \( y(-1) = 1 - 3 = -2 \). Negative leading coefficient (top-left to bottom-right), so the right-hand turning point is the maximum and the left-hand one is the minimum.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Negative cubic; roots \(\displaystyle 0, \pm\sqrt{3}\); max \(\displaystyle (1,2)\); min \(\displaystyle (-1,-2)\)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}

\section*{Curve Sketching 66\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-066)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 66 (cg5-066)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Sketch \( y = \ln x \), showing the x-intercept and asymptote.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Key features}
\[\begin{gathered}
x\text{-int: } (1, 0); \quad \text{asymptote } x = 0; \quad \text{domain } x > 0
\end{gathered}\]
\( \ln x \) is defined only for \( x > 0 \), so the curve sits entirely to the right of the \( y \)-axis. \( \ln 1 = 0 \), giving the \( x \)-intercept at \( (1, 0) \). As \( x \to 0^+ \), \( \ln x \to -\infty \), giving the vertical asymptote \( x = 0 \). The curve increases (more slowly as \( x \) grows).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Increasing curve; \(\displaystyle x\)-int \(\displaystyle (1,0)\); asymptote \(\displaystyle x = 0\)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}

\section*{Curve Sketching 67\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-067)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 67 (cg5-067)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Sketch \( y = (x-1)^2(x+2) \), showing all intercepts and where the curve touches the axis.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Roots}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 1 \text{ (touches)}, \quad x = -2 \text{ (crosses)}
\end{gathered}\]
The factor \( (x-1)^2 \) gives a double root at \( x = 1 \) (curve touches the axis), and \( x + 2 \) gives a single root at \( x = -2 \) (curve crosses).

\textit{Step 2: y-intercept}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = (0-1)^2(0+2) = 1 \times 2 = 2
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( x = 0 \): \( (-1)^2 = 1 \) and \( (0+2) = 2 \). Positive leading coefficient gives a bottom-left to top-right cubic shape.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Cubic; touches at \(\displaystyle (1,0)\); crosses at \(\displaystyle (-2,0)\); \(\displaystyle y\)-int \(\displaystyle (0,2)\)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}

\section*{Curve Sketching 68\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-068)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 68 (cg5-068)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Sketch \( y = \sin x \) for \( 0 \leq x \leq 2\pi \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Key features}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Starts at 0, max 1 at } \frac{\pi}{2}, \text{ back to 0 at } \pi, \text{ min -1 at } \frac{3\pi}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
One full period of \( \sin x \) has length \( 2\pi \). It crosses the \( x \)-axis at \( 0, \pi, 2\pi \), peaks at \( y = 1 \) when \( x = \tfrac{\pi}{2} \), and troughs at \( y = -1 \) when \( x = \tfrac{3\pi}{2} \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Sine wave from \(\displaystyle 0\) to \(\displaystyle 2\pi\); max \(\displaystyle 1\), min \(\displaystyle -1\)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}

\section*{Curve Sketching 69\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-069)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 69 (cg5-069)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Given the graph of \( y = f(x) \) passes through \( (-1, 0) \), \( (0, 2) \), \( (3, 0) \), sketch \( y = 2f(x) \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Vertical stretch by factor 2}
\[\begin{gathered}
(-1, 0) \to (-1, 0); \quad (0, 2) \to (0, 4); \quad (3, 0) \to (3, 0)
\end{gathered}\]
\( y = 2f(x) \) multiplies every \( y \)-value by 2. Points on the \( x \)-axis stay fixed (since \( 2 \times 0 = 0 \)), so the roots are unchanged; the \( y \)-intercept doubles from 2 to 4.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Same roots; \(\displaystyle y\)-int now \(\displaystyle (0,4)\); stretched vertically by 2
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}

\section*{Curve Sketching 70\quad\small\texttt{(cg5-070)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Curve Sketching 70 (cg5-070)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{Sketch \( y = x^3 - 6x^2 + 9x \), showing all intercepts, stationary points, and the point of inflection.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Factorise}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = x(x-3)^2
\end{gathered}\]
Take out a common \( x \) to get \( x(x^2 - 6x + 9) \), and recognise \( x^2 - 6x + 9 = (x-3)^2 \) as a perfect square. The double factor means \( x = 3 \) is a double root.

\textit{Step 2: Stationary points}
\[\begin{gathered}
dy/dx = 3x^2 - 12x + 9 = 3(x-1)(x-3) = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate term by term and factor. Numbers multiplying to \(3\) and adding to \(-4\) (after dividing by 3 inside the bracket) are \(-1\) and \(-3\).

\textit{Step 3: Values}
\[\begin{gathered}
y(1) = 1 - 6 + 9 = 4; \quad y(3) = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute back into the original equation. Positive leading coefficient: the left stationary point \( (1, 4) \) is the maximum and the right one \( (3, 0) \) is the minimum (the double root sits exactly on the \( x \)-axis).

\textit{Step 4: Inflection}
\[\begin{gathered}
d^2y/dx^2 = 6x - 12 = 0 \implies x = 2; \quad y(2) = 2
\end{gathered}\]
A point of inflection occurs where the second derivative is zero and changes sign. \( y(2) = 8 - 24 + 18 = 2 \), so the inflection is at \( (2, 2) \), midway between the two turning points.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Cubic; roots \(\displaystyle 0, 3\) (repeated); max \(\displaystyle (1,4)\); min \(\displaystyle (3,0)\); inflection \(\displaystyle (2,2)\)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Sketch.}
